Yeah, um… I guess the main thing is I can’t seem to keep my attention on things I really need to do. Like, I’ll sit down with a pile of student essays or some curriculum document, fully meaning to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been looking at the same page.

It’s weird because I’m technically there, at my desk, pen in hand and everything, but my mind’s gone off to three other places. Sometimes it’s like I’ve read the same paragraph but haven’t actually taken any of it in. And then I feel annoyed with myself, because as a teacher I’m supposed to be able to focus on this stuff… I mean, I tell my students to stay on task all the time, which is a bit ironic, I suppose.

And then I feel annoyed with myself, because as a teacher I’m supposed to be able to focus on this stuff… I mean, I tell my students to stay on task all the time, which is a bit ironic, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or find yourself moving around when you need to stay still, like tapping your hands or feet, or feeling like you’re always 'on the go'?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, not really? I mean, I probably tap a pen sometimes, but I’m a teacher, so there’s always a whiteboard marker or keys or something in my hand, and then I’ll put it down somewhere stupid and spend five minutes looking for it—which is not what you asked, sorry.\n\nThe “always on the go” thing doesn’t feel like me exactly. I can sit still if I have to. I haven’t really noticed feeling physically restless in that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah, it’s… it’s more obvious with work stuff, I think. I mean, I probably tap a pen sometimes, but I’m a teacher, so there’s always a whiteboard marker or keys or something in my hand, and then I’ll put it down somewhere stupid and spend five minutes looking for it—which is not what you asked, sorry.\n\nThe “always on the go” thing doesn’t feel like me exactly. I can sit still if I have to. I haven’t really noticed feeling physically restless in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find your mind wandering or have trouble focusing during other activities, like reading a book for pleasure, watching a movie, or having a conversation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s… it’s more obvious with work stuff, I think. Like I came in because I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, genuinely intending to get through it, and then suddenly I realize I’ve been on the same page for ages. Maybe ten minutes, maybe more, and I’m sort of thinking about three other things instead of what’s in front of me.\n\nIt’s not even that I’m choosing not to do it. I’ll have the pen in my hand and everything, like I’m being productive, but I’m barely taking in the words. With other things… I mean, I suppose it can happen too, but the essays are the one that really made me think, okay, this is becoming a problem.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find your mind wandering or having trouble focusing during activities that aren't work-related, like reading a book for fun, watching TV, or even during conversations with friends or family?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sustained_Attention_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document fully intending to work, and within ten minutes I realize I’ve been staring at the same page, thinking about three other things, and barely taking in what’s in front of me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Yeah, I mean… it does happen, but the clearest example is still the work stuff. Like, I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or some curriculum document, really meaning to just get through it, and then within ten minutes I sort of come to and realize I’ve been staring at the same page.

Yeah, I’m trying to think of a good non-work example, and of course my brain goes straight back to school because that’s where it’s most annoying. I know you asked about friends or cooking or that sort of thing.

But the pattern is the same, really. The reason I came in is that I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, fully meaning to get through it, and then after maybe ten minutes I realise I’ve just been staring at the same page. I’m off thinking about three other things, and I’ve barely taken in any of what’s in front of me.

Outside work… I’m sure it happens, but I’m not pulling up a neat example right now. It’s more that the work one is so obvious because there’s a page in front of me proving I haven’t moved on.

I haven’t really noticed feeling physically restless in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find your mind wandering or have trouble focusing during other activities, like reading a book for pleasure, watching a movie, or having a conversation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s… it’s more obvious with work stuff, I think. Like I came in because I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, genuinely intending to get through it, and then suddenly I realize I’ve been on the same page for ages. Maybe ten minutes, maybe more, and I’m sort of thinking about three other things instead of what’s in front of me.\n\nIt’s not even that I’m choosing not to do it. I’ll have the pen in my hand and everything, like I’m being productive, but I’m barely taking in the words. With other things… I mean, I suppose it can happen too, but the essays are the one that really made me think, okay, this is becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering or having trouble focusing during activities that aren't work-related, like reading a book for fun, watching TV, or even during conversations with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it does happen, but the clearest example is still the work stuff. Like, I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or some curriculum document, really meaning to just get through it, and then within ten minutes I sort of come to and realize I’ve been staring at the same page.\n\nAnd my head’s off somewhere else completely—thinking about three other things—and I haven’t really taken in what’s in front of me. Which is frustrating, because I look like I’m working. Pen in hand, papers there, all very teacherly… but I’m not actually absorbing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to focus or having a wandering mind in settings outside of work, such as during conversations, chores at home, or while trying to relax?\n\nPatient: I suppose, yeah, though I keep coming back to the work example because that’s the one that made me actually notice it. But I look like I’m working, pen in hand and everything, and then I realize I haven’t absorbed a thing. So yes, my mind wanders, but I’m sorry, I know you asked about outside work—I’m just finding it easiest to explain with that example.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when this kind of distraction or mind-wandering has come up outside of work? Like during a conversation with a friend, while reading something for fun, or maybe even during a task like cooking or driving?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’m trying to think of a good non-work example, and of course my brain goes straight back to school because that’s where it’s most annoying. I know you asked about friends or cooking or that sort of thing.\n\nBut the pattern is the same, really. The reason I came in is that I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, fully meaning to get through it, and then after maybe ten minutes I realise I’ve just been staring at the same page. I’m off thinking about three other things, and I’ve barely taken in any of what’s in front of me.\n\nOutside work… I’m sure it happens, but I’m not pulling up a neat example right now. It’s more that the work one is so obvious because there’s a page in front of me proving I haven’t moved on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Um… I don’t know, not really? I mean, I’m probably not the most patient person in the world, but I don’t think of myself as someone who’s always blurting things out or cutting people off.

In school, you sort of have to wait your turn to speak anyway—well, actually as a teacher you’re usually the one managing everyone else talking over each other, which is its own whole thing. But for me personally, I’m not sure that’s the main issue. I might occasionally jump in if I think I’ll forget what I was going to say, but I don’t know if that’s more than anyone else does. So… no, I haven’t really noticed impulsive stuff like that being a big problem.

Sometimes it’s like I’ve read the same paragraph but haven’t actually taken any of it in. I can sit still if I have to. I haven’t really noticed feeling physically restless in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find your mind wandering or have trouble focusing during other activities, like reading a book for pleasure, watching a movie, or having a conversation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s… it’s more obvious with work stuff, I think. I don’t think so, no… I mean, weekends can be a bit odd because sometimes I sort of waste them, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think, “Right, I’m going to go out, do something useful,” and then suddenly it’s Sunday evening and I’ve mostly done laundry badly and looked at my phone too much.

Can you tell me what's been troubling you lately that brought you here today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess the main thing is I can’t seem to keep my attention on things I really need to do. Like, I’ll sit down with a pile of student essays or some curriculum document, fully meaning to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been looking at the same page.\n\nIt’s weird because I’m technically there, at my desk, pen in hand and everything, but my mind’s gone off to three other places. Sometimes it’s like I’ve read the same paragraph but haven’t actually taken any of it in. And then I feel annoyed with myself, because as a teacher I’m supposed to be able to focus on this stuff… I mean, I tell my students to stay on task all the time, which is a bit ironic, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or find yourself moving around when you need to stay still, like tapping your hands or feet, or feeling like you’re always 'on the go'?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, not really? I mean, I probably tap a pen sometimes, but I’m a teacher, so there’s always a whiteboard marker or keys or something in my hand, and then I’ll put it down somewhere stupid and spend five minutes looking for it—which is not what you asked, sorry.\n\nThe “always on the go” thing doesn’t feel like me exactly. I can sit still if I have to. I haven’t really noticed feeling physically restless in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find your mind wandering or have trouble focusing during other activities, like reading a book for pleasure, watching a movie, or having a conversation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s… it’s more obvious with work stuff, I think. Like I came in because I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, genuinely intending to get through it, and then suddenly I realize I’ve been on the same page for ages. Maybe ten minutes, maybe more, and I’m sort of thinking about three other things instead of what’s in front of me.\n\nIt’s not even that I’m choosing not to do it. I’ll have the pen in my hand and everything, like I’m being productive, but I’m barely taking in the words. With other things… I mean, I suppose it can happen too, but the essays are the one that really made me think, okay, this is becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering or having trouble focusing during activities that aren't work-related, like reading a book for fun, watching TV, or even during conversations with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it does happen, but the clearest example is still the work stuff. Like, I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or some curriculum document, really meaning to just get through it, and then within ten minutes I sort of come to and realize I’ve been staring at the same page.\n\nAnd my head’s off somewhere else completely—thinking about three other things—and I haven’t really taken in what’s in front of me. Which is frustrating, because I look like I’m working. Pen in hand, papers there, all very teacherly… but I’m not actually absorbing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to focus or having a wandering mind in settings outside of work, such as during conversations, chores at home, or while trying to relax?\n\nPatient: I suppose, yeah, though I keep coming back to the work example because that’s the one that made me actually notice it. I’m off thinking about three other things, and I’ve barely taken in any of what’s in front of me.\n\nOutside work… I’m sure it happens, but I’m not pulling up a neat example right now. It’s more that the work one is so obvious because there’s a page in front of me proving I haven’t moved on.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself acting on impulse, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or feeling like you can’t wait your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, not really? I mean, I’m probably not the most patient person in the world, but I don’t think of myself as someone who’s always blurting things out or cutting people off.\n\nIn school, you sort of have to wait your turn to speak anyway—well, actually as a teacher you’re usually the one managing everyone else talking over each other, which is its own whole thing. But for me personally, I’m not sure that’s the main issue. I might occasionally jump in if I think I’ll forget what I was going to say, but I don’t know if that’s more than anyone else does. So… no, I haven’t really noticed impulsive stuff like that being a big problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods where you felt down or hopeless most of the time, or where you were feeling unusually worried or on edge about things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I get stressed, but I suppose everyone does, especially at school—there’s always something due, or some new policy, or a parent email that suddenly becomes a whole thing.\n\nBut “down or hopeless most of the time” feels… no, I don’t think so. Sorry, I’m not sure if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days or weeks where you felt little pleasure or interest in things you usually enjoy, or where you felt down, sad, or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear “days or weeks” sort of way. I mean, I have evenings where I get home and don’t really want to do anything, but then that might just be because the day’s been long.\n\nLike, I’ll plan to read or watch something and then end up just sort of scrolling, but I wouldn’t say I feel sad or empty most of the time. It’s more… tired, maybe? Sorry, I’m probably going sideways with it. No, I haven’t noticed a proper stretch where I’ve lost interest in everything I normally enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any specific times outside of work, like on weekends or during vacations, where you felt down, sad, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… I mean, weekends can be a bit odd because sometimes I sort of waste them, if that makes sense. Not really… I mean, after a bad day at work I can definitely be fed up, but that feels more like I’m replaying a conversation with a pupil or thinking I handled something badly, and then I’ll sort of potter around the flat and not start anything properly.

Maybe ten minutes, maybe more, and I’m sort of thinking about three other things instead of what’s in front of me.\n\nIt’s not even that I’m choosing not to do it. I’ll have the pen in my hand and everything, like I’m being productive, but I’m barely taking in the words. With other things… I mean, I suppose it can happen too, but the essays are the one that really made me think, okay, this is becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering or having trouble focusing during activities that aren't work-related, like reading a book for fun, watching TV, or even during conversations with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it does happen, but the clearest example is still the work stuff. Like, I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or some curriculum document, really meaning to just get through it, and then within ten minutes I sort of come to and realize I’ve been staring at the same page.\n\nAnd my head’s off somewhere else completely—thinking about three other things—and I haven’t really taken in what’s in front of me. Which is frustrating, because I look like I’m working. Pen in hand, papers there, all very teacherly… but I’m not actually absorbing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to focus or having a wandering mind in settings outside of work, such as during conversations, chores at home, or while trying to relax?\n\nPatient: I suppose, yeah, though I keep coming back to the work example because that’s the one that made me actually notice it. I’ll sit down with essays or a curriculum document, properly intending to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page.\n\nAnd I’m not taking it in at all—I’m thinking about three other things, like something I need to remember for later, or… actually, sometimes it’s not even useful thoughts, it’s just noise. But I look like I’m working, pen in hand and everything, and then I realize I haven’t absorbed a thing. So yes, my mind wanders, but I’m sorry, I know you asked about outside work—I’m just finding it easiest to explain with that example.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when this kind of distraction or mind-wandering has come up outside of work? Like during a conversation with a friend, while reading something for fun, or maybe even during a task like cooking or driving?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’m trying to think of a good non-work example, and of course my brain goes straight back to school because that’s where it’s most annoying. I know you asked about friends or cooking or that sort of thing.\n\nBut the pattern is the same, really. The reason I came in is that I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, fully meaning to get through it, and then after maybe ten minutes I realise I’ve just been staring at the same page. I’m off thinking about three other things, and I’ve barely taken in any of what’s in front of me.\n\nOutside work… I’m sure it happens, but I’m not pulling up a neat example right now. It’s more that the work one is so obvious because there’s a page in front of me proving I haven’t moved on.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself acting on impulse, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or feeling like you can’t wait your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, not really? I mean, I’m probably not the most patient person in the world, but I don’t think of myself as someone who’s always blurting things out or cutting people off.\n\nIn school, you sort of have to wait your turn to speak anyway—well, actually as a teacher you’re usually the one managing everyone else talking over each other, which is its own whole thing. But for me personally, I’m not sure that’s the main issue. I might occasionally jump in if I think I’ll forget what I was going to say, but I don’t know if that’s more than anyone else does. So… no, I haven’t really noticed impulsive stuff like that being a big problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods where you felt down or hopeless most of the time, or where you were feeling unusually worried or on edge about things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I get stressed, but I suppose everyone does, especially at school—there’s always something due, or some new policy, or a parent email that suddenly becomes a whole thing.\n\nBut “down or hopeless most of the time” feels… no, I don’t think so. Not in a way I’ve noticed as a period of time, if that makes sense. And worried or on edge—maybe sometimes, but I’d probably describe it more as being busy or overloaded than properly anxious. Sorry, I’m not sure if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days or weeks where you felt little pleasure or interest in things you usually enjoy, or where you felt down, sad, or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear “days or weeks” sort of way. I mean, I have evenings where I get home and don’t really want to do anything, but then that might just be because the day’s been long.\n\nLike, I’ll plan to read or watch something and then end up just sort of scrolling, but I wouldn’t say I feel sad or empty most of the time. It’s more… tired, maybe? Sorry, I’m probably going sideways with it. No, I haven’t noticed a proper stretch where I’ve lost interest in everything I normally enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any specific times outside of work, like on weekends or during vacations, where you felt down, sad, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… I mean, weekends can be a bit odd because sometimes I sort of waste them, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think, “Right, I’m going to go out, do something useful,” and then suddenly it’s Sunday evening and I’ve mostly done laundry badly and looked at my phone too much.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I felt down or sad exactly, or that I stopped enjoying things. Holidays are similar—sometimes it takes me a few days to switch off from school stuff, because you’re still mentally going through lessons or emails or whether you forgot to print something, even when there’s nothing to print because it’s half term. So… no, I can’t think of a specific weekend or vacation where I lost interest in things I usually like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any specific situations, like after a bad day at work or during quiet times at home, where you’ve felt down, sad, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, after a bad day at work I can definitely be fed up, but that feels more like I’m replaying a conversation with a pupil or thinking I handled something badly, and then I’ll sort of potter around the flat and not start anything properly.\n\nQuiet times at home are tricky because I sometimes don’t know what to do with them—I’ll think I should enjoy the free time, and then somehow I’ve made tea, opened three tabs, checked the football scores for no reason, and it’s late. But I wouldn’t say I feel properly down or sad, or like I’ve lost interest in things I enjoy. It’s more… I don’t know, drifting? Maybe tired. I haven’t noticed a specific situation like that, no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that one, yes. Getting started can be a bit of a thing, but the following-through is probably the bit that catches me out more.

Like, if my department chair gives me three or four things after a meeting, I’ll usually start the first one, and I might even do most of it. I’ll have the document open, or I’ll write the notes, or whatever. But then the last little step—sending the file, updating the shared calendar, printing the handouts—somehow just doesn’t happen unless someone reminds me.

And it’s annoying because it’s not like I don’t know what needs doing. I’ll think, “I’ll do that in a minute,” and then I’m looking at something else, or someone asks me about Year 9, or I remember I need to check the photocopier paper—actually the photocopier at school is its own nightmare—but yeah, the main thing is those final steps just seem to slip away.

I mean, I probably tap a pen sometimes, but I’m a teacher, so there’s always a whiteboard marker or keys or something in my hand, and then I’ll put it down somewhere stupid and spend five minutes looking for it—which is not what you asked, sorry.\n\nThe “always on the go” thing doesn’t feel like me exactly. I can sit still if I have to. I haven’t really noticed feeling physically restless in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find your mind wandering or have trouble focusing during other activities, like reading a book for pleasure, watching a movie, or having a conversation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s… it’s more obvious with work stuff, I think. Like I came in because I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, genuinely intending to get through it, and then suddenly I realize I’ve been on the same page for ages. Maybe ten minutes, maybe more, and I’m sort of thinking about three other things instead of what’s in front of me.\n\nIt’s not even that I’m choosing not to do it. I’ll have the pen in my hand and everything, like I’m being productive, but I’m barely taking in the words. With other things… I mean, I suppose it can happen too, but the essays are the one that really made me think, okay, this is becoming a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your mind wandering or having trouble focusing during activities that aren't work-related, like reading a book for fun, watching TV, or even during conversations with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it does happen, but the clearest example is still the work stuff. Like, I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or some curriculum document, really meaning to just get through it, and then within ten minutes I sort of come to and realize I’ve been staring at the same page.\n\nAnd my head’s off somewhere else completely—thinking about three other things—and I haven’t really taken in what’s in front of me. Which is frustrating, because I look like I’m working. Pen in hand, papers there, all very teacherly… but I’m not actually absorbing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to focus or having a wandering mind in settings outside of work, such as during conversations, chores at home, or while trying to relax?\n\nPatient: I suppose, yeah, though I keep coming back to the work example because that’s the one that made me actually notice it. I’ll sit down with essays or a curriculum document, properly intending to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page.\n\nAnd I’m not taking it in at all—I’m thinking about three other things, like something I need to remember for later, or… actually, sometimes it’s not even useful thoughts, it’s just noise. But I look like I’m working, pen in hand and everything, and then I realize I haven’t absorbed a thing. So yes, my mind wanders, but I’m sorry, I know you asked about outside work—I’m just finding it easiest to explain with that example.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when this kind of distraction or mind-wandering has come up outside of work? Like during a conversation with a friend, while reading something for fun, or maybe even during a task like cooking or driving?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’m trying to think of a good non-work example, and of course my brain goes straight back to school because that’s where it’s most annoying. I know you asked about friends or cooking or that sort of thing.\n\nBut the pattern is the same, really. The reason I came in is that I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, fully meaning to get through it, and then after maybe ten minutes I realise I’ve just been staring at the same page. I’m off thinking about three other things, and I’ve barely taken in any of what’s in front of me.\n\nOutside work… I’m sure it happens, but I’m not pulling up a neat example right now. It’s more that the work one is so obvious because there’s a page in front of me proving I haven’t moved on.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself acting on impulse, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or feeling like you can’t wait your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, not really? I mean, I’m probably not the most patient person in the world, but I don’t think of myself as someone who’s always blurting things out or cutting people off.\n\nIn school, you sort of have to wait your turn to speak anyway—well, actually as a teacher you’re usually the one managing everyone else talking over each other, which is its own whole thing. But for me personally, I’m not sure that’s the main issue. I might occasionally jump in if I think I’ll forget what I was going to say, but I don’t know if that’s more than anyone else does. So… no, I haven’t really noticed impulsive stuff like that being a big problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods where you felt down or hopeless most of the time, or where you were feeling unusually worried or on edge about things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I get stressed, but I suppose everyone does, especially at school—there’s always something due, or some new policy, or a parent email that suddenly becomes a whole thing.\n\nBut “down or hopeless most of the time” feels… no, I don’t think so. Not in a way I’ve noticed as a period of time, if that makes sense. And worried or on edge—maybe sometimes, but I’d probably describe it more as being busy or overloaded than properly anxious. Sorry, I’m not sure if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days or weeks where you felt little pleasure or interest in things you usually enjoy, or where you felt down, sad, or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear “days or weeks” sort of way. I mean, I have evenings where I get home and don’t really want to do anything, but then that might just be because the day’s been long.\n\nLike, I’ll plan to read or watch something and then end up just sort of scrolling, but I wouldn’t say I feel sad or empty most of the time. It’s more… tired, maybe? Sorry, I’m probably going sideways with it. No, I haven’t noticed a proper stretch where I’ve lost interest in everything I normally enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any specific times outside of work, like on weekends or during vacations, where you felt down, sad, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… I mean, weekends can be a bit odd because sometimes I sort of waste them, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think, “Right, I’m going to go out, do something useful,” and then suddenly it’s Sunday evening and I’ve mostly done laundry badly and looked at my phone too much.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I felt down or sad exactly, or that I stopped enjoying things. Holidays are similar—sometimes it takes me a few days to switch off from school stuff, because you’re still mentally going through lessons or emails or whether you forgot to print something, even when there’s nothing to print because it’s half term. So… no, I can’t think of a specific weekend or vacation where I lost interest in things I usually like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any specific situations, like after a bad day at work or during quiet times at home, where you’ve felt down, sad, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, after a bad day at work I can definitely be fed up, but that feels more like I’m replaying a conversation with a pupil or thinking I handled something badly, and then I’ll sort of potter around the flat and not start anything properly.\n\nQuiet times at home are tricky because I sometimes don’t know what to do with them—I’ll think I should enjoy the free time, and then somehow I’ve made tea, opened three tabs, checked the football scores for no reason, and it’s late. But I wouldn’t say I feel properly down or sad, or like I’ve lost interest in things I enjoy. It’s more… I don’t know, drifting? Maybe tired. I haven’t noticed a specific situation like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found it hard to stay quiet or still during activities that require you to remain seated, such as during meetings, long car rides, or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I can sit through meetings if I have to—at school there are plenty of those, obviously, and some of them are… well, you know. But I don’t think I’m the person who’s obviously unable to stay seated or anything.\n\nLong car rides, I suppose I get a bit bored, but doesn’t everyone? I’ll look out the window or check the route if I’m not driving, and then I start thinking about whether I remembered to put petrol in or—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nWatching a film, I can usually stay put. I might get up for a drink or pause it at home, but I don’t think I’ve noticed it as a major problem, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to get started on tasks, keep things organized, or follow through to finish what you start, especially when they’re things you don’t find interesting or are difficult?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, yes. Getting started can be a bit of a thing, but the following-through is probably the bit that catches me out more.\n\nLike, if my department chair gives me three or four things after a meeting, I’ll usually start the first one, and I might even do most of it. I’ll have the document open, or I’ll write the notes, or whatever. But then the last little step—sending the file, updating the shared calendar, printing the handouts—somehow just doesn’t happen unless someone reminds me.\n\nAnd it’s annoying because it’s not like I don’t know what needs doing. I’ll think, “I’ll do that in a minute,” and then I’m looking at something else, or someone asks me about Year 9, or I remember I need to check the photocopier paper—actually the photocopier at school is its own nightmare—but yeah, the main thing is those final steps just seem to slip away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed any specific situations, like after a bad day at work or during quiet times at home, where you’ve felt down, sad, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, after a bad day at work I can definitely be fed up, but that feels more like I’m replaying a conversation with a pupil or thinking I handled something badly, and then I’ll sort of potter around the flat and not start anything properly.\n\nQuiet times at home are tricky because I sometimes don’t know what to do with them—I’ll think I should enjoy the free time, and then somehow I’ve made tea, opened three tabs, checked the football scores for no reason, and it’s late. But I wouldn’t say I feel properly down or sad, or like I’ve lost interest in things I enjoy. It’s more… I don’t know, drifting? Maybe tired. I haven’t noticed a specific situation like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found it hard to stay quiet or still during activities that require you to remain seated, such as during meetings, long car rides, or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I can sit through meetings if I have to—at school there are plenty of those, obviously, and some of them are… well, you know. But I don’t think I’m the person who’s obviously unable to stay seated or anything.\n\nLong car rides, I suppose I get a bit bored, but doesn’t everyone? I’ll look out the window or check the route if I’m not driving, and then I start thinking about whether I remembered to put petrol in or—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nWatching a film, I can usually stay put. I might get up for a drink or pause it at home, but I don’t think I’ve noticed it as a major problem, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to get started on tasks, keep things organized, or follow through to finish what you start, especially when they’re things you don’t find interesting or are difficult?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, yes. Getting started can be a bit of a thing, but the following-through is probably the bit that catches me out more.\n\nLike, if my department chair gives me three or four things after a meeting, I’ll usually start the first one, and I might even do most of it. I’ll have the document open, or I’ll write the notes, or whatever. But then the last little step—sending the file, updating the shared calendar, printing the handouts—somehow just doesn’t happen unless someone reminds me.\n\nAnd it’s annoying because it’s not like I don’t know what needs doing. I’ll think, “I’ll do that in a minute,” and then I’m looking at something else, or someone asks me about Year 9, or I remember I need to check the photocopier paper—actually the photocopier at school is its own nightmare—but yeah, the main thing is those final steps just seem to slip away.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think of any specific examples where you had trouble focusing or your mind wandered during non-work activities, like reading a book, watching TV, or talking with someone?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sustained_Attention_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document fully intending to work, and within ten minutes I realize I’ve been staring at the same page, thinking about three other things, and barely taking in what’s in front of me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I suppose the clearest example is reading, though it’s not always a novel or something at home. I mean, even with a stack of essays or some curriculum document, I’ll sit down fully meaning to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realise I’ve been staring at the same page and I’ve taken in almost none of it.

It’s like my eyes are doing the reading, but my head’s off somewhere else — thinking about three other things at once. Sometimes it’s something completely unrelated, like whether I replied to a message, or what I’m having for dinner, or some random bit of school admin. And then I have to go back and read the same paragraph again, which is irritating because I was technically sitting there the whole time.

I mean, I’m probably not the most patient person in the world, but I don’t think of myself as someone who’s always blurting things out or cutting people off.\n\nIn school, you sort of have to wait your turn to speak anyway—well, actually as a teacher you’re usually the one managing everyone else talking over each other, which is its own whole thing. But for me personally, I’m not sure that’s the main issue. I might occasionally jump in if I think I’ll forget what I was going to say, but I don’t know if that’s more than anyone else does. So… no, I haven’t really noticed impulsive stuff like that being a big problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods where you felt down or hopeless most of the time, or where you were feeling unusually worried or on edge about things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean… at home, yes, though the example that comes to mind is still sort of work-adjacent, sorry. It’s that same pattern where if I’ve been given a few things to sort out, I’ll start one, maybe even get nearly all the way through it, and then the last bit just sort of falls off.

I suppose the thing is, it’s not that I refuse to finish it. I’ll genuinely think, “Right, I’ll just do that in a minute,” and then somehow I’m onto something else.

Yeah, I think the closest example is reading things at home, although annoyingly my brain goes straight back to school stuff again.

Like, I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, genuinely intending to get through it — I’ve made a tea, I’ve cleared a space, sometimes I’ve even got different coloured pens out, which is probably me pretending I’m more organised than I am — and then after maybe ten minutes I realise I’ve just been looking at the same page.

I’ll have sort of moved my eyes over the words, but I haven’t actually taken anything in. My mind’s off thinking about three other things at once, and then I have to go back and reread it. It’s frustrating, because it feels like I was “doing” the reading, but not actually absorbing it.

Sometimes it’s like I’ve read the same paragraph but haven’t actually taken any of it in. I can sit still if I have to. I mean, I’m probably not the most patient person in the world, but I don’t think of myself as someone who’s always blurting things out or cutting people off.\n\nIn school, you sort of have to wait your turn to speak anyway—well, actually as a teacher you’re usually the one managing everyone else talking over each other, which is its own whole thing. But for me personally, I’m not sure that’s the main issue. I might occasionally jump in if I think I’ll forget what I was going to say, but I don’t know if that’s more than anyone else does. So… no, I haven’t really noticed impulsive stuff like that being a big problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods where you felt down or hopeless most of the time, or where you were feeling unusually worried or on edge about things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I get stressed, but I suppose everyone does, especially at school—there’s always something due, or some new policy, or a parent email that suddenly becomes a whole thing.\n\nBut “down or hopeless most of the time” feels… no, I don’t think so. Not in a way I’ve noticed as a period of time, if that makes sense. And worried or on edge—maybe sometimes, but I’d probably describe it more as being busy or overloaded than properly anxious. Sorry, I’m not sure if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days or weeks where you felt little pleasure or interest in things you usually enjoy, or where you felt down, sad, or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear “days or weeks” sort of way. I mean, I have evenings where I get home and don’t really want to do anything, but then that might just be because the day’s been long.\n\nLike, I’ll plan to read or watch something and then end up just sort of scrolling, but I wouldn’t say I feel sad or empty most of the time. It’s more… tired, maybe? Sorry, I’m probably going sideways with it. No, I haven’t noticed a proper stretch where I’ve lost interest in everything I normally enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any specific times outside of work, like on weekends or during vacations, where you felt down, sad, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… I mean, weekends can be a bit odd because sometimes I sort of waste them, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think, “Right, I’m going to go out, do something useful,” and then suddenly it’s Sunday evening and I’ve mostly done laundry badly and looked at my phone too much.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I felt down or sad exactly, or that I stopped enjoying things. Holidays are similar—sometimes it takes me a few days to switch off from school stuff, because you’re still mentally going through lessons or emails or whether you forgot to print something, even when there’s nothing to print because it’s half term. So… no, I can’t think of a specific weekend or vacation where I lost interest in things I usually like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any specific situations, like after a bad day at work or during quiet times at home, where you’ve felt down, sad, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, after a bad day at work I can definitely be fed up, but that feels more like I’m replaying a conversation with a pupil or thinking I handled something badly, and then I’ll sort of potter around the flat and not start anything properly.\n\nQuiet times at home are tricky because I sometimes don’t know what to do with them—I’ll think I should enjoy the free time, and then somehow I’ve made tea, opened three tabs, checked the football scores for no reason, and it’s late. But I wouldn’t say I feel properly down or sad, or like I’ve lost interest in things I enjoy. It’s more… I don’t know, drifting? Maybe tired. I haven’t noticed a specific situation like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found it hard to stay quiet or still during activities that require you to remain seated, such as during meetings, long car rides, or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I can sit through meetings if I have to—at school there are plenty of those, obviously, and some of them are… well, you know. But I don’t think I’m the person who’s obviously unable to stay seated or anything.\n\nLong car rides, I suppose I get a bit bored, but doesn’t everyone? I’ll look out the window or check the route if I’m not driving, and then I start thinking about whether I remembered to put petrol in or—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nWatching a film, I can usually stay put. I might get up for a drink or pause it at home, but I don’t think I’ve noticed it as a major problem, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to get started on tasks, keep things organized, or follow through to finish what you start, especially when they’re things you don’t find interesting or are difficult?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, yes. I’ll have the document open, or I’ll write the notes, or whatever. I’ll think, “I’ll do that in a minute,” and then I’m looking at something else, or someone asks me about Year 9, or I remember I need to check the photocopier paper—actually the photocopier at school is its own nightmare—but yeah, the main thing is those final steps just seem to slip away.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any specific examples where you had trouble focusing or your mind wandered during non-work activities, like reading a book, watching TV, or talking with someone?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose the clearest example is reading, though it’s not always a novel or something at home. And then I have to go back and read the same paragraph again, which is irritating because I was technically sitting there the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times outside of work, like when you're at home or doing something unrelated to school, where you've had trouble finishing things you start or keeping things organized?\n\nPatient: I mean… at home, yes, though the example that comes to mind is still sort of work-adjacent, sorry. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I know you’re asking outside of work, and I’m trying to think of a purely home example, but the one that keeps coming to mind is after meetings at school, because it’s exactly that sort of thing.

My department chair will give me, say, three or four little jobs to do — not massive things, just “send this file,” “put that date in the shared calendar,” “print the handouts,” that kind of thing. And I’ll start properly. I’ll open the document, make the notes, maybe even get most of it done, and then the last step just… doesn’t happen.

It’s not that I decide not to do it. I’ll think, “Right, I’ll send that in a minute,” and then I’m distracted by something else, or I move on to the next bit, or I’m thinking about something totally different. Then later someone has to remind me, and I feel a bit ridiculous because it’s usually such a small final step.

Can you tell me what's been troubling you lately that brought you here today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess the main thing is I can’t seem to keep my attention on things I really need to do. Like, I’ll sit down with a pile of student essays or some curriculum document, fully meaning to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been looking at the same page.\n\nIt’s weird because I’m technically there, at my desk, pen in hand and everything, but my mind’s gone off to three other places. Sometimes it’s like I’ve read the same paragraph but haven’t actually taken any of it in. And then I feel annoyed with myself, because as a teacher I’m supposed to be able to focus on this stuff… I mean, I tell my students to stay on task all the time, which is a bit ironic, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or find yourself moving around when you need to stay still, like tapping your hands or feet, or feeling like you’re always 'on the go'?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, not really? I mean, I probably tap a pen sometimes, but I’m a teacher, so there’s always a whiteboard marker or keys or something in my hand, and then I’ll put it down somewhere stupid and spend five minutes looking for it—which is not what you asked, sorry.\n\nThe “always on the go” thing doesn’t feel like me exactly. I can sit still if I have to. I haven’t really noticed feeling physically restless in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you also find your mind wandering or have trouble focusing during other activities, like reading a book for pleasure, watching a movie, or having a conversation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s… it’s more obvious with work stuff, I think. Like I came in because I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, genuinely intending to get through it, and then suddenly I realize I’ve been on the same page for ages. Maybe ten minutes, maybe more, and I’m sort of thinking about three other things instead of what’s in front of me.\n\nIt’s not even that I’m choosing not to do it. I’ll have the pen in my hand and everything, like I’m being productive, but I’m barely taking in the words. Like, I’ll sit down with a stack of essays or some curriculum document, really meaning to just get through it, and then within ten minutes I sort of come to and realize I’ve been staring at the same page.\n\nAnd my head’s off somewhere else completely—thinking about three other things—and I haven’t really taken in what’s in front of me. Which is frustrating, because I look like I’m working. Pen in hand, papers there, all very teacherly… but I’m not actually absorbing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to focus or having a wandering mind in settings outside of work, such as during conversations, chores at home, or while trying to relax?\n\nPatient: I suppose, yeah, though I keep coming back to the work example because that’s the one that made me actually notice it. I’ll sit down with essays or a curriculum document, properly intending to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page.\n\nAnd I’m not taking it in at all—I’m thinking about three other things, like something I need to remember for later, or… actually, sometimes it’s not even useful thoughts, it’s just noise. But I look like I’m working, pen in hand and everything, and then I realize I haven’t absorbed a thing. So yes, my mind wanders, but I’m sorry, I know you asked about outside work—I’m just finding it easiest to explain with that example.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time when this kind of distraction or mind-wandering has come up outside of work? Like during a conversation with a friend, while reading something for fun, or maybe even during a task like cooking or driving?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’m trying to think of a good non-work example, and of course my brain goes straight back to school because that’s where it’s most annoying. I know you asked about friends or cooking or that sort of thing.\n\nBut the pattern is the same, really. The reason I came in is that I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, fully meaning to get through it, and then after maybe ten minutes I realise I’ve just been staring at the same page. I’m off thinking about three other things, and I’ve barely taken in any of what’s in front of me.\n\nOutside work… I’m sure it happens, but I’m not pulling up a neat example right now. It’s more that the work one is so obvious because there’s a page in front of me proving I haven’t moved on.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself acting on impulse, like interrupting others, blurting out answers, or feeling like you can’t wait your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, not really? I mean, I’m probably not the most patient person in the world, but I don’t think of myself as someone who’s always blurting things out or cutting people off.\n\nIn school, you sort of have to wait your turn to speak anyway—well, actually as a teacher you’re usually the one managing everyone else talking over each other, which is its own whole thing. But for me personally, I’m not sure that’s the main issue. I might occasionally jump in if I think I’ll forget what I was going to say, but I don’t know if that’s more than anyone else does. So… no, I haven’t really noticed impulsive stuff like that being a big problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods where you felt down or hopeless most of the time, or where you were feeling unusually worried or on edge about things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I get stressed, but I suppose everyone does, especially at school—there’s always something due, or some new policy, or a parent email that suddenly becomes a whole thing.\n\nBut “down or hopeless most of the time” feels… no, I don’t think so. Not in a way I’ve noticed as a period of time, if that makes sense. And worried or on edge—maybe sometimes, but I’d probably describe it more as being busy or overloaded than properly anxious. Sorry, I’m not sure if that answers what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days or weeks where you felt little pleasure or interest in things you usually enjoy, or where you felt down, sad, or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear “days or weeks” sort of way. I mean, I have evenings where I get home and don’t really want to do anything, but then that might just be because the day’s been long.\n\nLike, I’ll plan to read or watch something and then end up just sort of scrolling, but I wouldn’t say I feel sad or empty most of the time. It’s more… tired, maybe? Sorry, I’m probably going sideways with it. No, I haven’t noticed a proper stretch where I’ve lost interest in everything I normally enjoy.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any specific times outside of work, like on weekends or during vacations, where you felt down, sad, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… I mean, weekends can be a bit odd because sometimes I sort of waste them, if that makes sense. Like I’ll think, “Right, I’m going to go out, do something useful,” and then suddenly it’s Sunday evening and I’ve mostly done laundry badly and looked at my phone too much.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I felt down or sad exactly, or that I stopped enjoying things. Holidays are similar—sometimes it takes me a few days to switch off from school stuff, because you’re still mentally going through lessons or emails or whether you forgot to print something, even when there’s nothing to print because it’s half term. So… no, I can’t think of a specific weekend or vacation where I lost interest in things I usually like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any specific situations, like after a bad day at work or during quiet times at home, where you’ve felt down, sad, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, after a bad day at work I can definitely be fed up, but that feels more like I’m replaying a conversation with a pupil or thinking I handled something badly, and then I’ll sort of potter around the flat and not start anything properly.\n\nQuiet times at home are tricky because I sometimes don’t know what to do with them—I’ll think I should enjoy the free time, and then somehow I’ve made tea, opened three tabs, checked the football scores for no reason, and it’s late. But I wouldn’t say I feel properly down or sad, or like I’ve lost interest in things I enjoy. It’s more… I don’t know, drifting? Maybe tired. I haven’t noticed a specific situation like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found it hard to stay quiet or still during activities that require you to remain seated, such as during meetings, long car rides, or watching a movie?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I mean, I can sit through meetings if I have to—at school there are plenty of those, obviously, and some of them are… well, you know. But I don’t think I’m the person who’s obviously unable to stay seated or anything.\n\nLong car rides, I suppose I get a bit bored, but doesn’t everyone? I’ll look out the window or check the route if I’m not driving, and then I start thinking about whether I remembered to put petrol in or—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nWatching a film, I can usually stay put. I might get up for a drink or pause it at home, but I don’t think I’ve noticed it as a major problem, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to get started on tasks, keep things organized, or follow through to finish what you start, especially when they’re things you don’t find interesting or are difficult?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one, yes. Getting started can be a bit of a thing, but the following-through is probably the bit that catches me out more.\n\nLike, if my department chair gives me three or four things after a meeting, I’ll usually start the first one, and I might even do most of it. I’ll have the document open, or I’ll write the notes, or whatever. But then the last little step—sending the file, updating the shared calendar, printing the handouts—somehow just doesn’t happen unless someone reminds me.\n\nAnd it’s annoying because it’s not like I don’t know what needs doing. I’ll think, “I’ll do that in a minute,” and then I’m looking at something else, or someone asks me about Year 9, or I remember I need to check the photocopier paper—actually the photocopier at school is its own nightmare—but yeah, the main thing is those final steps just seem to slip away.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any specific examples where you had trouble focusing or your mind wandered during non-work activities, like reading a book, watching TV, or talking with someone?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose the clearest example is reading, though it’s not always a novel or something at home.
